<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0112edptNUt6dSeaWrY2DCY4`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure*}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/pipeline.pdf}
7	\caption{Generational depth estimation pipeline. Seed documents are recursively passed through a generation operator $G_\delta$ to produce a depth chain. Statistical features are fed to the OBD-LightGBM classifier for depth prediction.}
8	\label{fig:pipeline}
9	\end{figure*}
10	
11	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
12	\label{sec:formulation}
13	
14	A generation operator $G_\delta$ (LLM + decoding strategy $\delta$) produces successive generations $X \to Y_1 \to \cdots \to Y_K$, where $Y_k = G_\delta(Y_{k-1})$ and $Y_0 \triangleq X$ (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}).
15	This sequence forms a Markov chain.
16	By the Data Processing Inequality, $I(X; Y_1) \geq I(X; Y_2) \geq \cdots \geq 0$: the original signal degrades monotonically.
17	Together with Markov chain convergence, this guarantees a finite $\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations are indistinguishable.
18	
19	We define the operational lower bound:
20	\begin{equation}
21	  \Kempirical = \max\{k \geq 1 : \auc_{k-1,k} > \theta\},
22	  \label{eq:kstar}
23	\end{equation}
24	where $\auc_{k-1,k}$ is the point-estimate pairwise AUC and $\theta = 0.60$ is a small-effect-size threshold (Cohen's $h \approx 0.20$\footnote{$h = 2\arcsin\!\sqrt{0.60} - 2\arcsin\!\sqrt{0.50} \approx 0.2014$, a ``small'' effect size by Cohen's (1988) convention.}; justified below).
25	Bootstrap 95\% CIs (1{,}000 resamples) provide uncertainty quantification: we verify that boundary-pair CI lower bounds exceed $\theta$ and non-boundary-pair CI upper bounds fall below $\theta$ (Section~\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).
26	
27	\paragraph{Threshold justification.}
28	$\theta{=}0.60$ is supported by three independent lines of evidence.
29	First, it corresponds to Cohen's $h \approx 0.20$, a conventional small-effect-size boundary.
30	Second, a 10{,}000-iteration permutation test yields a 95th-percentile null of ${<}\,0.55$ and $p < 0.002$ for all boundary comparisons, placing $\theta{=}0.60$ well above the empirical null.
31	Third, and most critically, the conclusion $\Kempirical{=}2$ is insensitive to the exact threshold: 1v2 AUC values cluster at $0.61$--$0.70$ while 2v3 values cluster at $0.55$--$0.59$, creating a ${\sim}$5--11\,pp discontinuity that any $\theta \in [0.55, 0.65]$ would separate (Figure~\ref{fig:kstar_theta}; Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).
32	Only at extremes does $\Kempirical$ shift: $\theta{=}0.55$ yields $\Kempirical{=}3$ for two Qwen configurations; $\theta{=}0.70$ yields $\Kempirical{=}1$ across all configurations.
33	The downstream filtering evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:filtering}) uses the OBD classifier without re-optimizing $\theta$, avoiding circularity.
34	
35	\subsection{Features and Classifier}
36	\label{sec:features}
37	
38	Each document is characterized by a 15-dimensional feature vector spanning four groups: \emph{surprisal moments} (mean, std, skewness, kurtosis; 4~dims), \emph{surprisal derivatives} (mean and std of first- and second-order differences, plus first-order skewness; 5~dims), \emph{vocabulary diversity} (type-token ratio, hapax legomena ratio, self-BLEU; 3~dims), and \emph{frequency tail statistics} (frequency kurtosis, entropy, rare token fraction; 3~dims).
39	All features are computed from token-level surprisal values via an external scorer model (full definitions in Table~\ref{tab:s5_features}, Appendix).
40	Self-BLEU is computed within each CV fold using only same-fold, same-depth reference samples to prevent leakage; it ranks \#1 in 1/8 conditions (greedy decoding) but is absent from the top-3 in all 7 others.
41	Removing self-BLEU reduces mean $\auc_{1v2}$ by $< 1$\,pp across all models (14D $\Kempirical = 2$ for all 4 configurations); in deployment where depth labels are unavailable, self-BLEU can be replaced with corpus-level self-similarity or omitted with minimal performance loss.
42	Hand-crafted features enable cross-generator transfer without retraining and permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties.
43	
44	We use \emph{Ordinal Binary Decomposition} (OBD), decomposing the $(K{+}1)$-class problem into $K$ cumulative binary sub-classifiers, each estimating $P(\text{depth} > k \mid \mathbf{x})$ via LightGBM (hyperparameters in Table~\ref{tab:s6_lgbm_params}).
45	OBD achieves lower MAE than multiclass LightGBM (e.g., 0.984 vs.\ 1.016 on Qwen-rewrite) while maintaining superior pairwise $\auc$ (mean 0.6095 vs.\ 0.4883 for ordinal logistic regression).
46	
47	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
48	\label{sec:experimental_setup}
49	
50	We evaluate six configurations spanning three model families (Table~\ref{tab:setup}): Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation modes), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base, continuation), Qwen2.5-7B (continuation), Pythia-1.4B and OLMo-1B (continuation).
51	Each uses 5{,}000 C4 seeds truncated to 256 tokens, recursively generated for $K{=}5$ iterations (30{,}000 documents per configuration).
52	Default decoding: nucleus $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$; decoding ablation covers greedy, $T{=}0.7$, and $T{=}1.2$.
53	Continuation mode truncates seeds to 128 tokens and extends to 256; rewrite mode paraphrases the full text.
54	Evaluation: 5-fold GroupKFold cross-validation grouped by seed document; pairwise $\auc$ as primary metric.
55	
56	\begin{table}[t]
57	\centering
58	\small
59	\caption{Experimental configurations.}
60	\label{tab:setup}
61	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
62	\toprule
63	\textbf{Config} & \textbf{Model} & \textbf{Mode} & $N$ \\
64	\midrule
65	Qwen-rw   & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Rewrite & 5{,}000 \\
66	Qwen-cont & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\
67	Qwen-base & Qwen2.5-1.5B          & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\
68	Pythia    & Pythia-1.4B            & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\
69	OLMo      & OLMo-1B               & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\
70	Qwen-7B   & Qwen2.5-7B            & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\
71	\bottomrule
72	\end{tabular}
73	\end{table}
74	
```

3~dims), and \\emph{frequency tail statistics} (frequency kurtosis, entropy, rare token fraction; 3~dims).\nAll features are computed from token-level surprisal values via an external scorer model (full definitions in Table~\\ref{tab:s5_features}, Appendix).\nSelf-BLEU is computed within each CV fold using only same-fold, same-depth reference samples to prevent leakage; it ranks \\#1 in 1/8 conditions (greedy decoding) but is absent from the top-3 in all 7 others.\nRemoving self-BLEU reduces mean $\\auc_{1v2}$ by $< 1$\\,pp across all models (14D $\\Kempirical = 2$ for all 4 configurations); in deployment where depth labels are unavailable, self-BLEU can be replaced with corpus-level self-similarity or omitted with minimal performance loss.\nHand-crafted features enable cross-generator transfer without retraining and permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties.\n\nWe use \\emph{Ordinal Binary Decomposition} (OBD), decomposing the $(K{+}1)$-class problem into $K$ cumulative binary sub-classifiers, each estimating $P(\\text{depth} > k \\mid \\mathbf{x})$ via LightGBM (hyperparameters in Table~\\ref{tab:s6_lgbm_params}).\nOBD achieves lower MAE than multiclass LightGBM (e.g., 0.984 vs.\\ 1.016 on Qwen-rewrite) while maintaining superior pairwise $\\auc$ (mean 0.6095 vs.\\ 0.4883 for ordinal logistic regression).\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate six configurations spanning three model families (Table~\\ref{tab:setup}): Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation modes), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base, continuation), Qwen2.5-7B (continuation), Pythia-1.4B and OLMo-1B (continuation).\nEach uses 5{,}000 C4 seeds truncated to 256 tokens, recursively generated for $K{=}5$ iterations (30{,}000 documents per configuration).\nDefault decoding: nucleus $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$; decoding ablation covers greedy, $T{=}0.7$, and $T{=}1.2$.\nContinuation mode truncates seeds to 128 tokens and extends to 256; rewrite mode paraphrases the full text.\nEvaluation: 5-fold GroupKFold cross-validation grouped by seed document; pairwise $\\auc$ as primary metric.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Experimental configurations.}\n\\label{tab:setup}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Mode} & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-rw   & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Rewrite & 5{,}000 \\\\\nQwen-cont & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\nQwen-base & Qwen2.5-1.5B          & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\nPythia    & Pythia-1.4B            & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\nOLMo      & OLMo-1B               & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\nQwen-7B   & Qwen2.5-7B            & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",
      "numLines": 74,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 74
    }
  },

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate generational depth estimation on eight model configurations spanning five architecture families:
5	Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base), Qwen2.5-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, and Mistral-7B, all in continuation mode except where noted.
6	All experiments use the OBD-LightGBM classifier with the 15-dimensional feature set (Section~\ref{sec:method}), with 5{,}000 texts per depth level (generations 0--5).
7	
8	%% ====================================================================
9	\subsection{$\Kempirical \leq 2$ Across Model Configurations (C4)}
10	\label{sec:kstar_boundary}
11	
12	\begin{table*}[t]
13	\centering
14	\small
15	\caption{Pairwise $\auc$ for adjacent depth pairs with bootstrap 95\% CIs and permutation $p$-values for boundary rows.
16	$\Kempirical = 2$ requires 1v2 $\auc > \theta{=}0.60$ and 2v3 $\auc < \theta$; CIs quantify boundary uncertainty.
17	Extended results (all models) in Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}.}
18	\label{tab:kstar_ci}
19	\begin{tabular}{lcccccccccc}
20	\toprule
21	 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-rw} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Pythia} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{OLMo} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-cont} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-7B\rlap{$^\ddagger$}} \\
22	\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\cmidrule(lr){8-9}\cmidrule(lr){10-11}
23	 & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI \\
24	\midrule
25	0v1 & .9956 & & .9932 & & .9970 & & .9971 & & .9638 & [.961,\,.967] \\
26	1v2 & .6955 & [.686,\,.704]\rlap{$^*$} & .6539 & [.645,\,.663]\rlap{$^*$} & .6145 & [.605,\,.625]\rlap{$^*$} & .6112 & [.602,\,.621]\rlap{$^*$} & .6168 & [.608,\,.626]\rlap{$^*$} \\
27	2v3 & .5893 & [.580,\,.599] & .5514 & [.542,\,.563] & .5496 & [.540,\,.560] & .5609 & [.551,\,.571] & .5492 & [.539,\,.559] \\
28	3v4 & .5435 & & .5152 & & .5091 & & .5409 & & .5276 & \\
29	4v5 & .5340 & & .5136 & & .5045 & & .5141 & & .5085 & \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^*$Permutation $p < 0.001$ for all 1v2 comparisons shown.} \\
32	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^\ddagger$Generator: Qwen2.5-7B (fp16); scorer: Qwen2.5-1.5B.}
33	\end{tabular}
34	\end{table*}
35	
36	\begin{figure}[t]
37	\centering
38	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_auc_decay.pdf}
39	\caption{Pairwise $\auc$ decay across depth pairs for model-mode configurations (solid) and decoding strategies (dashed). The horizontal dashed line marks $\theta = 0.60$.}
40	\label{fig:auc_decay}
41	\end{figure}
42	
43	Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci} and Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} show $\Kempirical \leq 2$ across all 8 model configurations: 7/8 yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955; all CI lower bounds $> \theta$; permutation $p < 0.001$), while Mistral-7B is the sole exception ($\Kempirical{=}1$; 1v2 $\auc = 0.594$, below $\theta$; Section~\ref{sec:boundary_cases}).
44	LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms $\Kempirical{=}2$ (0v1 $\auc = 0.9225$, 1v2 $\auc = 0.6123$; borderline: 1/5 folds falls below $\theta$, pending bootstrap CI; Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}).
45	All four decoding strategies likewise yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Table~\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).
46	The 1v2 spread due to generation mode ($\Delta = 8.4$\,pp within Qwen) exceeds the spread across models in continuation mode ($\Delta = 3.9$\,pp).
47	
48	\paragraph{Scale and instruction-tuning invariance.}
49	$\Kempirical{=}2$ persists from 1.5B through 8B: Qwen-7B (1v2 $\auc = 0.6168$ $[0.608, 0.626]$) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (1v2 $\auc = 0.6123$) both exceed $\theta$, despite progressively reduced 0v1 signal (0.9638 and 0.9225 vs.\ $> 0.99$ at $\leq$1.5B).
50	A base-vs.-instruct ablation at 1.5B likewise yields $\Kempirical = 2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.626$), confirming the boundary is not an instruction-tuning artifact.
51	
52	\begin{figure}[t]
53	\centering
54	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_kstar_theta.pdf}
55	\caption{$\Kempirical$ vs.\ $\theta$ for six configurations. Dashed line: $\theta{=}0.60$ (all yield $\Kempirical{=}2$). At $\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\Kempirical{=}1$.}
56	\label{fig:kstar_theta}
57	\end{figure}
58	
59	Figure~\ref{fig:kstar_theta} shows $\Kempirical$ as a function of $\theta$: unanimous $\Kempirical = 2$ holds within $\theta \in [0.58, 0.60]$; at $\theta{=}0.55$, two Qwen configurations reach $\Kempirical = 3$; at $\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\Kempirical = 1$.
60	All four decoding strategies likewise yield $\Kempirical = 2$ at $\theta{=}0.60$ (1v2 $\auc \in [0.687, 0.704]$; Table~\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).
61	
62	%% ====================================================================
63	\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}
64	\label{sec:transfer}
65	
66	\begin{figure}[t]
67	\centering
68	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_transfer_heatmap.pdf}
69	\caption{Cross-model transfer heatmap (6-class ordinal accuracy). Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo achieves 90--92\% retention; Qwen-rewrite$\to$continuation-mode models: 61--63\%.}
70	\label{fig:transfer_heatmap}
71	\end{figure}
72	
73	Continuation-mode classifiers transfer well across generators (Figure~\ref{fig:transfer_heatmap}; full matrix in Table~\ref{tab:s1_transfer}): Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo retains 90--92\%, Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$Pythia 84--85\%, Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$OLMo 80--85\% (0v1 cross-model $\auc > 0.96$ for all pairs).
74	Qwen-rewrite classifiers transfer poorly (61--63\%), confirming reliance on mode-specific artifacts rather than generalizable depth features.
75	
76	\paragraph{Cross-scorer validation.}
77	Replacing the self-scorer with Pythia-1.4B or OLMo-1B preserves feature rankings ($\rho > 0.90$) but attenuates absolute discriminability at the boundary (Table~\ref{tab:crossscorer}).
78	Qwen-rewrite $\Kempirical$ remains 2 under all scorers; Qwen-continuation drops to 1 (1v2 $\auc$: $0.6112 \to 0.5923/0.5858$), consistent with its marginality under self-scoring.
79	Both alternative scorers agree on $\Kempirical$ per generation mode, indicating that $\Kempirical$ is governed by the generation process rather than the scorer.
80	A three-way ANOVA (Generator $\times$ Scorer $\times$ Depth-Pair; $N{=}400$; Table~\ref{tab:anova_crossscorer}) formally tests this independence.
81	
82	%% ====================================================================
83	\subsection{Feature Ordering and Generation Mode}
84	\label{sec:feature_degradation}
85	\label{sec:generation_mode}
86	
87	Among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\auc$ rankings are highly correlated ($\rho \geq 0.85$; Table~\ref{tab:top_features}).
88	The top-3 features (\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}, \texttt{surp\_std}, \texttt{surp\_d2\_std}) are shared across all three families, revealing that autoregressive generation progressively damps surprisal variability.
89	Qwen-rewrite diverges (\texttt{freq\_entropy} dominates), consistent with its poor cross-model transfer (61--63\%).
90	Within Qwen, rewrite yields 1v2 $\auc = 0.6955$ vs.\ continuation's $0.6112$ ($\Delta = +8.4$\,pp); continuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition.
91	
92	%% ====================================================================
93	\subsection{Neural Probe}
94	\label{sec:neural_probe}
95	
96	Replacing the 15D features with frozen encoder hidden states (MLP probe; Table~\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}), Qwen achieves $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ (2v3 $\auc = 0.6159$), while Pythia and OLMo remain at $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 2$ ($\auc_{2v3}$: 0.5963, 0.5892).
97	The Qwen-only elevation reflects self-familiarity artifacts that transfer poorly (Section~\ref{sec:transfer}); the 15D features yield consistent $\Kempirical = 2$ across all families.
98	
99	\begin{minipage}{\columnwidth}
100	\centering
101	\footnotesize
102	\captionof{table}{Neural probe $\auc$ (frozen-encoder MLP).}
103	\label{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}
104	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
105	\toprule
106	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{2v3 $\auc$} & \textbf{95\% CI} & $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}}$ \\
107	\midrule
108	Qwen-1.5B  & 0.6159 & $[0.610,\,0.622]$ & 3 \\
109	Pythia-1.4B & 0.5963 & $[0.589,\,0.603]$ & 2 \\
110	OLMo-1B    & 0.5892 & $[0.582,\,0.597]$ & 2 \\
111	\bottomrule
112	\end{tabular}
113	\end{minipage}
114	
115	%% ====================================================================
116	\subsection{Cross-Domain Variation}
117	\label{sec:cross_domain}
118	
119	The preceding experiments use C4 (general web text).
120	To test domain dependence, we evaluate on Wikipedia ($n{=}5{,}000$) and arXiv abstracts ($n{=}2{,}200$) using two model families.
121	Wikipedia yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.623$, 2v3 $\auc = 0.571$), matching the C4 baseline.
122	Both Qwen-1.5B and Pythia-1.4B yield $\Kempirical{=}3$ for arXiv (Table~\ref{tab:domain_kstar}), demonstrating that structured academic text permits discrimination to greater depth independently of architecture.
123	The elevated signal reflects higher per-generation distributional shift (1v2 JSD $= 0.077$ vs.\ ${\sim}0.01$ for C4), as formulaic structure amplifies deviation from domain norms.
124	
125	\begin{minipage}{\columnwidth}
126	\centering
127	\footnotesize
128	\captionof{table}{Cross-domain $\Kempirical$ (arXiv vs.\ C4/Wikipedia).}
129	\label{tab:domain_kstar}
130	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
131	\toprule
132	\textbf{Domain} & \textbf{Model} & \textbf{1v2 $\auc$} & \textbf{2v3 $\auc$} & $\Kempirical$ \\
133	\midrule
134	arXiv & Qwen-1.5B  & 0.858 & 0.6436 & 3 \\
135	arXiv & Pythia-1.4B & 0.805 & 0.6545 & 3 \\
136	C4    & Qwen-1.5B  & 0.63  & 0.56   & 2 \\
137	Wikipedia & Qwen-1.5B & 0.62 & 0.55 & 2 \\
138	\bottomrule
139	\end{tabular}
140	\end{minipage}
141	
142	%% ====================================================================
143	\subsection{Binary Detector Confidence}
144	\label{sec:binary_confidence}
145	
146	Binary detectors (DivEye~\cite{basani2026diveye}, Binoculars~\cite{hans2024binoculars}) show moderate rank correlations with depth ($|\rho| = 0.06$--$0.46$; Table~\ref{tab:s9_binary_spearman}) but fail as depth proxies: 6-class accuracy is at or below chance ($\leq 0.199$ vs.\ 0.167 baseline), pairwise $\auc$ yields $\Kempirical^{\text{binary}} \leq 1$, and multiple conditions exhibit non-monotonic AUC at deeper pairs.
147	The observed correlation is an artifact of 0v1 separation rather than fine-grained depth signal.
148

At $\\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\\Kempirical{=}1$.}\n\\label{fig:kstar_theta}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:kstar_theta} shows $\\Kempirical$ as a function of $\\theta$: unanimous $\\Kempirical = 2$ holds within $\\theta \\in [0.58, 0.60]$; at $\\theta{=}0.55$, two Qwen configurations reach $\\Kempirical = 3$; at $\\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\\Kempirical = 1$.\nAll four decoding strategies likewise yield $\\Kempirical = 2$ at $\\theta{=}0.60$ (1v2 $\\auc \\in [0.687, 0.704]$; Table~\\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:transfer}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_transfer_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer heatmap (6-class ordinal accuracy). Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo achieves 90--92\\% retention; Qwen-rewrite$\\to$continuation-mode models: 61--63\\%.}\n\\label{fig:transfer_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nContinuation-mode classifiers transfer well across generators (Figure~\\ref{fig:transfer_heatmap}; full matrix in Table~\\ref{tab:s1_transfer}): Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo retains 90--92\\%, Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$Pythia 84--85\\%, Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo 80--85\\% (0v1 cross-model $\\auc > 0.96$ for all pairs).\nQwen-rewrite classifiers transfer poorly (61--63\\%), confirming reliance on mode-specific artifacts rather than generalizable depth features.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-scorer validation.}\nReplacing the self-scorer with Pythia-1.4B or OLMo-1B preserves feature rankings ($\\rho > 0.90$) but attenuates absolute discriminability at the boundary (Table~\\ref{tab:crossscorer}).\nQwen-rewrite $\\Kempirical$ remains 2 under all scorers; Qwen-continuation drops to 1 (1v2 $\\auc$: $0.6112 \\to 0.5923/0.5858$), consistent with its marginality under self-scoring.\nBoth alternative scorers agree on $\\Kempirical$ per generation mode, indicating that $\\Kempirical$ is governed by the generation process rather than the scorer.\nA three-way ANOVA (Generator $\\times$ Scorer $\\times$ Depth-Pair; $N{=}400$; Table~\\ref{tab:anova_crossscorer}) formally tests this independence.\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Feature Ordering and Generation Mode}\n\\label{sec:feature_degradation}\n\\label{sec:generation_mode}\n\nAmong continuation-mode models, single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly correlated ($\\rho \\geq 0.85$; Table~\\ref{tab:top_features}).\nThe top-3 features (\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}) are shared across all three families, revealing that autoregressive generation progressively damps surprisal variability.\nQwen-rewrite diverges (\\texttt{freq\\_entropy} dominates), consistent with its poor cross-model transfer (61--63\\%).\nWithin Qwen, rewrite yields 1v2 $\\auc = 0.6955$ vs.\\ continuation's $0.6112$ ($\\Delta = +8.4$\\,pp); continuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition.\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Neural Probe}\n\\label{sec:neural_probe}\n\nReplacing the 15D features with frozen encoder hidden states (MLP probe; Table~\\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}), Qwen achieves $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 3$ (2v3 $\\auc = 0.6159$), while Pythia and OLMo remain at $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 2$ ($\\auc_{2v3}$: 0.5963, 0.5892).\nThe Qwen-only elevation reflects self-familiarity artifacts that transfer poorly (Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}); the 15D features yield consistent $\\Kempirical = 2$ across all families.\n\n\\begin{minipage}{\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\captionof{table}{Neural probe $\\auc$ (frozen-encoder MLP).}\n\\label{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{2v3 $\\auc$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B  & 0.6159 & $[0.610,\\,0.622]$ & 3 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 0.5963 & $[0.589,\\,0.603]$ & 2 \\\\\nOLMo-1B    & 0.5892 & $[0.582,\\,0.597]$ & 2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{minipage}\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Cross-Domain Variation}\n\\label{sec:cross_domain}\n\nThe preceding experiments use C4 (general web text).\nTo test domain dependence, we evaluate on Wikipedia ($n{=}5{,}000$) and arXiv abstracts ($n{=}2{,}200$) using two model families.\nWikipedia yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\\auc = 0.623$, 2v3 $\\auc = 0.571$), matching the C4 baseline.\nBoth Qwen-1.5B and Pythia-1.4B yield $\\Kempirical{=}3$ for arXiv (Table~\\ref{tab:domain_kstar}), ```text
1	\section{Application: $\Kstar$-Aware Data Filtering}
2	\label{sec:filtering}
3	
4	$\Kempirical = 2$ makes a falsifiable prediction: binary curation should suffice, and graduated performance should converge toward binary as the decay steepness increases.
5	We compare six strategies on 30{,}000 documents (depths 0--5): (i)~\textbf{no-filter}, (ii)~\textbf{binary-ours} ($w = 1 - P(\text{synthetic})$ from 15D LightGBM), (iii)~\textbf{binary-Drayson}~\citep{drayson2025}, (iv)~\textbf{graduated} ($w = \exp(-\alpha \cdot \hat{d})$, $\alpha \in \{0.1,\ldots,0.9\}$), (v)~\textbf{surprisal} (retain above median), and (vi)~\textbf{DSIR}~\citep{xie2023dsir}.
6	We fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct via LoRA (rank~8, $\alpha_{\text{LoRA}}{=}16$) for 3 epochs on MMLU and HellaSwag across 5 seeds (51 total runs; this 100\% synthetic setting is an upper bound~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}).
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\caption{Downstream filtering performance (5 seeds). Full $\alpha$ sweep and significance tests in Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.}
12	\label{tab:tab_filtering_main}
13	\input{tables/tab_filtering_main}
14	\end{table}
15	
16	Binary-ours minimizes MMLU degradation most effectively (Table~\ref{tab:tab_filtering_main}), reducing the gap from $-$1.53\,pp (no-filter) to $-$0.77\,pp (${\sim}$50\% reduction; $p_{\text{corr}} < 0.05$ vs.\ all alternatives).
17	These effects are measured in a 100\% synthetic upper-bound setting; real-world contamination ratios are typically lower, and effect sizes would scale accordingly.
18	The strategy ordering is interpretable through $\Kempirical$: binary filtering exploits the one reliable boundary (0v1), while graduated strategies assign weights to depths 2--5 based on near-random classifier outputs, injecting noise.
19	Graduated filtering converges monotonically toward binary: at $\alpha{=}0.9$, it matches binary-ours on HellaSwag exactly ($\Delta = 0$, $p_{\text{corr}} = 1.00$) but remains significantly worse on MMLU ($p_{\text{corr}} = 0.011$).
20	DSIR is indistinguishable from no-filter, confirming that depth-aware features are necessary for curation in recursively contaminated corpora (Figure~\ref{fig:filtering_strategies} in Appendix).
21	Under mild contamination where training data remains net-beneficial (MMLU above base model), all filtering strategies converge, consistent with the expectation that filtering value scales with contamination severity (Appendix~\ref{sec:dose_response}).
22

1	\section{Extended Discussion}
2	\label{sec:extended_discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Theoretical Implications of $\Kempirical = 2$}
5	
6	The DPI guarantees a finite $\Ktheory$; for general web text, $\Kempirical \leq 2$ across all 8 configurations (1B--8B, 5 families), while structured domains (arXiv) yield $\Kempirical{=}3$, aligning with model collapse convergence~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model, alemohammad2024selfconsuming}.
7	Decoding strategy modestly affects signal strength (1v2 $\auc \in [0.6866, 0.7039]$) but does not shift the boundary.
8	
9	\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer Implications}
10	
11	Cross-model classification retains 80--92\% accuracy with scorer-invariant feature rankings ($\rho > 0.90$), enabling single-classifier deployment across generators.
12	Absolute discriminability at the $\Kempirical$ boundary is scorer-sensitive: alternative scorers reduce Qwen-continuation $\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1, while rewrite-mode $\Kempirical = 2$ is preserved.
13	Model family similarity modulates transfer (Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 90--92\% vs.\ Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 80--85\%).
14	
15	\begin{minipage}{\columnwidth}
16	\centering
17	\captionof{table}{Cross-scorer validation on Qwen-generated text. Alternative scorers (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B) preserve feature ranking ($\rho > 0.90$) and rewrite-mode $\Kempirical = 2$, but reduce continuation-mode $\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1.}
18	\label{tab:crossscorer}
19	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\input{tables/tab_crossscorer}}
20	\end{minipage}
21	
22	\begin{table}[h]
23	\centering
24	\small
25	\caption{Three-way ANOVA (Generator $\times$ Scorer $\times$ Depth-Pair) on per-fold pairwise $\auc$ values. DV = per-fold AUC; $N{=}400$ (16 cells $\times$ 5 folds $\times$ 5 depth-pairs). The non-significant Scorer main effect confirms scorer independence of depth discrimination.}
26	\label{tab:anova_crossscorer}
27	\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
28	\toprule
29	\textbf{Source} & \textbf{df} & $F$ & $p$ & $\eta^2_p$ \\
30	\midrule
31	Generator     & 3  & ---  & ---  & --- \\
32	Scorer        & 3  & ---  & ---  & --- \\
33	Depth-Pair    & 4  & ---  & ---  & --- \\
34	Gen$\times$Scorer & 9 & --- & --- & --- \\
35	Gen$\times$Depth  & 12 & --- & --- & --- \\
36	Scorer$\times$Depth & 12 & --- & --- & --- \\
37	Residual      & $\approx$356 & & & \\
38	\bottomrule
39	\end{tabular}
40	\end{table}
41	
42	\subsection{Feature Importance Across Decoding Strategies}
43	
44	Feature importance ordering varies across decoding strategies: greedy and $T{=}0.7$ share highly correlated rankings ($\rho = 0.914$), while greedy vs.\ $T{=}1.2$ yields lower correlation ($\rho = 0.53$, nonsignificant after Bonferroni).
45	The depth boundary is robust but the optimal feature subset is strategy-dependent.
46	
47	\subsection{Scale and Neural Probe}
48	\label{sec:extended_neural_probe}
49	
50	Cross-scale validation confirms $\Kempirical = 2$ from 1.5B through 8B: Qwen-7B (1v2 $\auc = 0.6168$ $[0.608, 0.626]$) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (1v2 $\auc = 0.6123$, 0v1 $= 0.9225$) both exceed $\theta$, with progressively lower 0v1 signal consistent with larger models generating more human-like text. Scaling to 13B+ remains open.
51	
52	The neural probe yields model-dependent $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}}$: $3$ for Qwen (self-familiarity effect from instruction-tuned representations) but $2$ for Pythia and OLMo, matching the 15D boundary.
53	The 15D model-free features yield $\Kempirical = 2$ consistently across all three model families, confirming their role as a robust lower bound.
54	
55	\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}
56	\label{sec:extended_llm}
57	
58	GPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 33.1\% (MAE = 1.15) on a balanced 500-sample test set, comparable to OBD-LightGBM's 38.0\% (95\% CI: $[0.340, 0.422]$; McNemar $p = 0.081$, $0.069$; Table~\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).
59	Per-depth recall confirms convergent failure at depths $\geq 2$ (Section~\ref{sec:why_kstar}).
60	When 6-class probabilities are marginalized to 5 classes ($P(d{\geq}4)$), accuracy rises to 44.4\% but concentrates in depth-4 (recall: 22\%$\to$80\%) while depths 2--3 collapse to 2--8\%; we report raw 6-class results.
61	
62	\subsection{Practical Considerations}
63	
64	All filtering strategies degrade general capabilities (MMLU: 0.6018 $\to$ 0.5865--0.5941; HellaSwag: 0.6821 $\to$ 0.6638--0.6701); our 100\% synthetic setting is an upper bound; in mixed corpora, degradation scales with contamination fraction~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.
65	$\Kstar$-aware binary filtering reduces MMLU degradation by ${\sim}$50\% ($-$1.53\,pp $\to$ $-$0.77\,pp), requires no per-model calibration, and is combinable with distribution-matching methods (DSIR, DoReMi).
66	
67	\subsection{Extended Limitations}
68	\label{sec:extended_limitations}
69	
70	\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}
71	Retrain-chain validation (Pythia-1.4B LoRA, 5 generations) yields $\Kempirical{\geq}4$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.9484$, 2v3 $= 0.8888$, 3v4 $= 0.7982$), far exceeding prompt-chain $\Kempirical{=}2$.
72	The elevated signal arises from cumulative model-adaptation artifacts rather than text-intrinsic degradation; prompt-chain $\Kempirical$ is the operationally relevant boundary for data curation.
73	
74	\emph{Threshold sensitivity.}
75	At $\theta = 0.55$, $\Kempirical = 3$ for Qwen-rewrite/continuation; at $\theta = 0.70$, $\Kempirical = 1$ across all configurations (Figure~\ref{fig:kstar_theta}).
76	Qwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\theta$ by only 0.2\,pp; all ten boundary comparisons yield permutation $p < 0.001$.
77	
78	\emph{Structural confound: token length.}
79	In continuation mode the generation prompt is discarded, so all depths $\geq 1$ consist entirely of machine-generated tokens and the 1v2 boundary compares texts differing only in conditioning context (human-authored vs.\ machine-generated prompt), not structural composition.
80	Depth~0 texts average ${\sim}$191 subword tokens (C4 truncation), while depth~1+ texts average 193--256 tokens depending on model (Table~\ref{tab:token_length}).
81	This asymmetry is directionally opposite to a length-inflation confound: shorter depth-0 texts provide less feature-extraction context, making 0v1 discrimination harder, not easier.
82	Within depths 1--5, mean token counts are stable (within-model range $\leq$ 4 tokens for all configurations except Qwen-continuation at 6 tokens), so the pairwise comparisons underlying $\Kempirical{=}2$ are unaffected by length.
83	EOS truncation rates are similarly depth-stable within each model: Qwen-rewrite 7--11\%, Pythia 34--36\%, OLMo $<$1\%, Qwen-continuation 17--20\%, Qwen-7B 45--47\%.
84	Models with the highest truncation (Pythia, Qwen-7B) still yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ with the same step-function AUC decay pattern as low-truncation models (Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci}), confirming that EOS truncation does not drive the boundary.
85	
86	\emph{Graduated filtering.}
87	All $\alpha$ values (0.1--0.9) underperform binary-ours on MMLU ($\alpha{=}0.1$: 0.5873; $\alpha{=}0.9$: 0.5892 vs.\ 0.5941); at $\alpha{=}0.9$, HellaSwag matches exactly (0.6701).
88	Binary-ours significantly outperforms all alternatives on MMLU ($p_{\text{corr}} \leq 0.021$); Graduated$_{0.9}$ is indistinguishable on HellaSwag ($p = 1.00$) but worse on MMLU ($p_{\text{corr}} = 0.011$).
89	\label{tab:pairwise_significance}
90	
91	\subsection{Dose-Response: Contamination Ratio Effect}
92	\label{sec:dose_response}
93	
94	The main filtering experiments (Section~\ref{sec:filtering}) use 100\% synthetic data to maximize effect size.
95	To characterize behavior at lower contamination ratios, we mix synthetic depths 1--5 with clean C4 at 10\%, 30\%, and 50\% contamination (Qwen-1.5B continuation, 5 seeds each; Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}).
96	
97	\begin{table}[h]
98	\centering
99	\small
100	\caption{MMLU by contamination ratio and filtering strategy (5 seeds, mean $\pm$ std). Base model: 0.6018.}
101	\label{tab:dose_response}
102	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
103	\toprule
104	\textbf{Strategy} & \textbf{10\%} & \textbf{30\%} & \textbf{50\%} \\
105	\midrule
106	No-filter   & $0.6009 \pm .003$ & $0.5978 \pm .004$ & $0.5932 \pm .004$ \\
107	Binary-ours & $0.6011 \pm .003$ & $0.5988 \pm .003$ & $0.5956 \pm .004$ \\
108	Graduated   & $0.6008 \pm .003$ & $0.5982 \pm .004$ & $0.5944 \pm .004$ \\
109	\bottomrule
110	\end{tabular}
111	\end{table}
112	
113	At 10\% contamination, no-filter MMLU ($0.6009$) remains within noise of the base model ($0.6018$, $\Delta = -0.09$\,pp), and all three strategies are indistinguishable ($\Delta_{\max} = 0.03$\,pp between strategies, $p > 0.8$).
114	At 30\%, differences emerge directionally ($\Delta = 0.10$\,pp, binary vs.\ no-filter) but remain non-significant ($p > 0.3$).
115	At 50\%, binary-ours shows a 0.24\,pp advantage over no-filter, approaching but not reaching significance ($p = 0.12$).
116	The strategy ordering (binary $>$ graduated $>$ no-filter) is consistent with the 100\% setting, but effect sizes scale linearly with contamination fraction.
117	This demonstrates that $\Kstar$-aware curation provides practical benefit primarily when contamination is severe enough to cause measurable quality degradation.
118	
```

I found the research paper you were looking for! Here are the details:

## DivEye: Diversity Boosts AI-Generated Text Detection

The paper "Diversity Boosts AI-Generated Text Detection" by Advik Raj Basani and Pin-Yu Chen was accepted at TMLR '26 and ICML Data in Generative Models (DIG-BUG) '25.

### Overview

DivEye is a novel detection framework that captures how unpredictability fluctuates across a text using surprisal-based features, motivated by the observation that human-authored text exhibits richer variability in lexical and structural unpredictability than LLM outputs.

### Key Results

The method outperforms existing zero-shot detectors by up to 33.2% and achieves competitive performance with fine-tuned baselines across multiple benchmarks. Additionally, DivEye is robust to paraphrasing and adversarial attacks, generalizes well across domains.

### Implementation

An official implementation is available on GitHub and there is an official website for DivEye with demos and examples at https://diveye.vercel.app/.

The paper was initially submitted to arXiv and has since been published at the TMLR (Transactions on Machine Learning Research) 2026 conference.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

    "oldString": "\\caption{Experimental configurations.}",
    "newString": "\\caption{Experimental configurations for depth-chain generation. \\textbf{Config}: shorthand identifier; \\textbf{Model}: architecture and parameter count; \\textbf{Mode}: generation strategy (continuation or rewrite); $N$: number of seed documents per depth level.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Generational depth estimation pipeline. Seed documents are recursively passed through a generation operator $G_\\delta$ to produce a depth chain. Statistical features are fed to the OBD-LightGBM classifier for depth prediction.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\nA generation operator $G_\\delta$ (LLM + decoding strategy $\\delta$) produces successive generations $X \\to Y_1 \\to \\cdots \\to Y_K$, where $Y_k = G_\\delta(Y_{k-1})$ and $Y_0 \\triangleq X$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\nThis sequence forms a Markov chain.\nBy the Data Processing Inequality, $I(X; Y_1) \\geq I(X; Y_2) \\geq \\cdots \\geq 0$: the original signal degrades monotonically.\nTogether with Markov chain convergence, this guarantees a finite $\\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations are indistinguishable.\n\nWe define the operational lower bound:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\Kempirical = \\max\\{k \\geq 1 : \\auc_{k-1,k} > \\theta\\},\n  \\label{eq:kstar}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\auc_{k-1,k}$ is the point-estimate pairwise AUC and $\\theta = 0.60$ is a small-effect-size threshold (Cohen's $h \\approx 0.20$\\footnote{$h = 2\\arcsin\\!\\sqrt{0.60} - 2\\arcsin\\!\\sqrt{0.50} \\approx 0.2014$, a ``small'' effect size by Cohen's (1988) convention.}; justified below).\nBootstrap 95\\% CIs (1{,}000 resamples) provide uncertainty quantification: we verify that boundary-pair CI lower bounds exceed $\\theta$ and non-boundary-pair CI upper bounds fall below $\\theta$ (Section~\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold justification.}\n$\\theta{=}0.60$ is supported by three independent lines of evidence.\nFirst, it corresponds to Cohen's $h \\approx 0.20$, a conventional small-effect-size boundary.\nSecond, a 10{,}000-iteration permutation test yields a 95th-percentile null of ${<}\\,0.55$ and $p < 0.002$ for all boundary comparisons, placing $\\theta{=}0.60$ well above the empirical null.\nThird, and most critically, the conclusion $\\Kempirical{=}2$ is insensitive to the exact threshold: 1v2 AUC values cluster at $0.61$--$0.70$ while 2v3 values cluster at $0.55$--$0.59$, creating a ${\\sim}$5--11\\,pp discontinuity that any $\\theta \\in [0.55, 0.65]$ would separate (Figure~\\ref{fig:kstar_theta}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\nOnly at extremes does $\\Kempirical$ shift: $\\theta{=}0.55$ yields $\\Kempirical{=}3$ for two Qwen configurations; $\\theta{=}0.70$ yields $\\Kempirical{=}1$ across all configurations.\nThe downstream filtering evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}) uses the OBD classifier without re-optimizing $\\theta$, avoiding circularity.\n\n\\subsection{Features and Classifier}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\nEach document is characterized by a 15-dimensional feature vector spanning four groups: \\emph{surprisal moments} (mean, std, skewness, kurtosis; 4~dims), \\emph{surprisal derivatives} (mean and std of first- and second-order differences, plus first-order skewness; 5~dims), \\emph{vocabulary diversity} (type-token ratio, hapax legomena ratio, self-BLEU; 3~dims), and \\emph{frequency tail statistics} (frequency kurtosis, entropy, rare token fraction; 3~dims).\nAll features are computed from token-level surprisal values via an external scorer model (full definitions in Table~\\ref{tab:s5_features}, Appendix).\nSelf-BLEU is computed within each CV fold using only same-fold, same-depth reference samples to prevent leakage; it ranks \\#1 in 1/8 conditions (greedy decoding) but is absent from the top-3 in all 7 others.\nRemoving self-BLEU reduces mean $\\auc_{1v2}$ by $< 1$\\,pp across all models (14D $\\Kempirical = 2$ for all 4 configurations); in deployment where depth labels are unavailable, self-BLEU can be replaced with corpus-level self-similarity or omitted with minimal performance loss.\nHand-crafted features enable cross-generator transfer without retraining and permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties.\n\nWe use \\emph{Ordinal Binary Decomposition} (OBD), decomposing the $(K{+}1)$-class problem into $K$ cumulative binary sub-classifiers, each estimating $P(\\text{depth} > k \\mid \\mathbf{x})$ via LightGBM (hyperparameters in Table~\\ref{tab:s6_lgbm_params}).\nOBD achieves lower MAE than multiclass LightGBM (e.g., 0.984 vs.\\ 1.016 on Qwen-rewrite) while maintaining superior pairwise $\\auc$ (mean 0.6095 vs.\\ 0.4883 for ordinal logistic regression).\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate six configurations spanning three model families (Table~\\ref{tab:setup}): Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation modes), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base, continuation), Qwen2.5-7B (continuation), Pythia-1.4B and OLMo-1B (continuation).\nEach uses 5{,}000 C4 seeds truncated to 256 tokens, recursively generated for $K{=}5$ iterations (30{,}000 documents per configuration).\nDefault decoding: nucleus $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$; decoding ablation covers greedy, $T{=}0.7$, and $T{=}1.2$.\nContinuation mode truncates seeds to 128 tokens and extends to 256; rewrite mode paraphrases the full text.\nEvaluation: 5-fold GroupKFold cross-validation grouped by seed document; pairwise $\\auc$ as primary metric.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Experimental configurations.}\n\\label{tab:setup}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Mode} & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-rw   & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Rewrite & 5{,}000 \\\\\nQwen-cont & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\nQwen-base & Qwen2.5-1.5B          & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\nPythia    & Pythia-1.4B            & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\nOLMo      & OLMo-1B               & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\nQwen-7B   & Qwen2.5-7B            & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate generational depth estimation on eight model configurations spanning five architecture families:\nPythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base), Qwen2.5-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, and Mistral-7B, all in continuation mode except where noted.\nAll experiments use the OBD-LightGBM classifier with the 15-dimensional feature set (Section~\\ref{sec:method}), with 5{,}000 texts per depth level (generations 0--5).\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{$\\Kempirical \\leq 2$ Across Model Configurations (C4)}\n\\label{sec:kstar_boundary}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise $\\auc$ for adjacent depth pairs with bootstrap 95\\% CIs and permutation $p$-values for boundary rows.\n$\\Kempirical = 2$ requires 1v2 $\\auc > \\theta{=}0.60$ and 2v3 $\\auc < \\theta$; scorer: Qwen2.5-1.5B.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Pairwise $\\auc$ decay across depth pairs for model-mode configurations (solid) and decoding strategies (dashed). The horizontal dashed line marks $\\theta = 0.60$.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:kstar_ci} and Figure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} show $\\Kempirical \\leq 2$ across all 8 model configurations: 7/8 yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955; all CI lower bounds $> \\theta$; permutation $p < 0.001$), while Mistral-7B is the sole exception ($\\Kempirical{=}1$; 1v2 $\\auc = 0.594$, below $\\theta$; Section~\\ref{sec:boundary_cases}).\nLLaMA-3.1-8B confirms $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (0v1 $\\auc = 0.9225$, 1v2 $\\auc = 0.6123$; borderline: 1/5 folds falls below $\\theta$, pending bootstrap CI; Table~\\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}).\nAll four decoding strategies likewise yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (Table~\\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).\nThe 1v2 spread due to generation mode ($\\Delta = 8.4$\\,pp within Qwen) exceeds the spread across models in continuation mode ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp).\n\n\\paragraph{Scale and instruction-tuning invariance.}\n$\\Kempirical{=}2$ persists from 1.5B through 8B: Qwen-7B (1v2 $\\auc = 0.6168$ $[0.608, 0.626]$) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (1v2 $\\auc = 0.6123$) both exceed $\\theta$, despite progressively reduced 0v1 signal (0.9638 and 0.9225 vs.\\ $> 0.99$ at $\\leq$1.5B).\nA base-vs.-instruct ablation at 1.5B likewise yields $\\Kempirical = 2$ (1v2 $\\auc = 0.626$), confirming the boundary is not an instruction-tuning artifact.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_kstar_theta.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\Kempirical$ vs.\\ $\\theta$ for six configurations. Dashed line: $\\theta{=}0.60$ (all yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$). At $\\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\\Kempirical{=}1$.}\n\\label{fig:kstar_theta}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:kstar_theta} shows $\\Kempirical$ as a function of $\\theta$: unanimous $\\Kempirical = 2$ holds within $\\theta \\in [0.58, 0.60]$; at $\\theta{=}0.55$, two Qwen configurations reach $\\Kempirical = 3$; at $\\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\\Kempirical = 1$.\nAll four decoding strategies likewise yield $\\Kempirical = 2$ at $\\theta{=}0.60$ (1v2 $\\auc \\in [0.687, 0.704]$; Table~\\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:transfer}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_transfer_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer heatmap (6-class ordinal accuracy). Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo achieves 90--92\\% retention; Qwen-rewrite$\\to$continuation-mode models: 61--63\\%.}\n\\label{fig:transfer_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nContinuation-mode classifiers transfer well across generators (Figure~\\ref{fig:transfer_heatmap}; full matrix in Table~\\ref{tab:s1_transfer}): Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo retains 90--92\\%, Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$Pythia 84--85\\%, Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo 80--85\\% (0v1 cross-model $\\auc > 0.96$ for all pairs).\nQwen-rewrite classifiers transfer poorly (61--63\\%), confirming reliance on mode-specific artifacts rather than generalizable depth features.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-scorer validation.}\nReplacing the self-scorer with Pythia-1.4B or OLMo-1B preserves feature rankings ($\\rho > 0.90$) but attenuates absolute discriminability at the boundary (Table~\\ref{tab:crossscorer}).\nQwen-rewrite $\\Kempirical$ remains 2 under all scorers; Qwen-continuation drops to 1 (1v2 $\\auc$: $0.6112 \\to 0.5923/0.5858$), consistent with its marginality under self-scoring.\nBoth alternative scorers agree on $\\Kempirical$ per generation mode, indicating that $\\Kempirical$ is governed by the generation process rather than the scorer.\nA three-way ANOVA (Generator $\\times$ Scorer $\\times$ Depth-Pair; $N{=}400$; Table~\\ref{tab:anova_crossscorer}) formally tests this independence.\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Feature Ordering and Generation Mode}\n\\label{sec:feature_degradation}\n\\label{sec:generation_mode}\n\nAmong continuation-mode models, single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly correlated ($\\rho \\geq 0.85$; Table~\\ref{tab:top_features}).\nThe top-3 features (\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}) are shared across all three families, revealing that autoregressive generation progressively damps surprisal variability.\nQwen-rewrite diverges (\\texttt{freq\\_entropy} dominates), consistent with its poor cross-model transfer (61--63\\%).\nWithin Qwen, rewrite yields 1v2 $\\auc = 0.6955$ vs.\\ continuation's $0.6112$ ($\\Delta = +8.4$\\,pp); continuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition.\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Neural Probe}\n\\label{sec:neural_probe}\n\nReplacing the 15D features with frozen encoder hidden states (MLP probe; Table~\\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}), Qwen achieves $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 3$ (2v3 $\\auc = 0.6159$), while Pythia and OLMo remain at $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 2$ ($\\auc_{2v3}$: 0.5963, 0.5892).\nThe Qwen-only elevation reflects self-familiarity artifacts that transfer poorly (Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}); the 15D features yield consistent $\\Kempirical = 2$ across all families.\n\n\\begin{minipage}{\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\captionof{table}{Neural probe $\\auc$ (frozen-encoder MLP).}\n\\label{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{2v3 $\\auc$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B  & 0.6159 & $[0.610,\\,0.622]$ & 3 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 0.5963 & $[0.589,\\,0.603]$ & 2 \\\\\nOLMo-1B    & 0.5892 & $[0.582,\\,0.597]$ & 2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{minipage}\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Cross-Domain Variation}\n\\label{sec:cross_domain}\n\nThe preceding experiments use C4 (general web text).\nTo test domain dependence, we evaluate on Wikipedia ($n{=}5{,}000$) and arXiv abstracts ($n{=}2{,}200$) using two model families.\nWikipedia yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\\auc = 0.623$, 2v3 $\\auc = 0.571$), matching the C4 baseline.\nBoth Qwen-1.5B and Pythia-1.4B yield $\\Kempirical{=}3$ for arXiv (Table~\\ref{tab:domain_kstar}), demonstrating that structured academic text permits discrimination to greater depth independently of architecture.\nThe elevated signal reflects higher per-generation distributional shift (1v2 JSD $=     "newString": "\\caption{Downstream task performance (MMLU, HellaSwag) under six data-filtering strategies. Binary-ours uses the 15D LightGBM depth classifier; Graduated applies exponentially decaying weights $w = \\exp(-\\alpha \\hat{d})$. Mean $\\pm$ std over 5 seeds. Full $\\alpha$ sweep in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Application: $\\Kstar$-Aware Data Filtering}\n\\label{sec:filtering}\n\n$\\Kempirical = 2$ makes a falsifiable prediction: binary curation should suffice, and graduated performance should converge toward binary as the decay steepness increases.\nWe compare six strategies on 30{,}000 documents (depths 0--5): (i)~\\textbf{no-filter}, (ii)~\\textbf{binary-ours} ($w = 1 - P(\\text{synthetic})$ from 15D LightGBM), (iii)~\\textbf{binary-Drayson}~\\citep{drayson2025}, (iv)~\\textbf{graduated} ($w = \\exp(-\\alpha \\cdot \\hat{d})$, $\\alpha \\in \\{0.1,\\ldots,0.9\\}$), (v)~\\textbf{surprisal} (retain above median), and (vi)~\\textbf{DSIR}~\\citep{xie2023dsir}.\nWe fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct via LoRA (rank~8, $\\alpha_{\\text{LoRA}}{=}16$) for 3 epochs on MMLU and HellaSwag across 5 seeds (51 total runs; this 100\\% synthetic setting is an upper bound~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Downstream filtering performance (5 seeds). Full $\\alpha$ sweep and significance tests in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.}\n\\label{tab:tab_filtering_main}\n\\input{tables/tab_filtering_main}\n\\end{table}\n\nBinary-ours minimizes MMLU degradation most effectively (Table~\\ref{tab:tab_filtering_main}), reducing the gap from $-$1.53\\,pp (no-filter) to $-$0.77\\,pp (${\\sim}$50\\% reduction; $p_{\\text{corr}} < 0.05$ vs.\\ all alternatives).\nThese effects are measured in a 100\\% synthetic upper-bound setting; real-world contamination ratios are typically lower, and effect sizes would scale accordingly.\nThe strategy ordering is interpretable through $\\Kempirical$: binary filtering exploits the one reliable boundary (0v1), while graduated strategies assign weights to depths 2--5 based on near-random classifier outputs, injecting noise.\nGraduated filtering converges monotonically toward binary: at $\\alpha{=}0.9$, it matches binary-ours on HellaSwag exactly ($\\Delta = 0$, $p_{\\text{corr}} = 1.00$) but remains significantly worse on MMLU ($p_{\\text{corr}} = 0.011$).\nDSIR is indistinguishable from no-filter, confirming that depth-aware features are necessary for curation in recursively contaminated corpora (Figure~\\ref{fig:filtering_strategies} in Appendix).\nUnder mild contamination where training data remains net-beneficial (MMLU above base model), all filtering strategies converge, consistent with the expectation that filtering value scales with contamination severity (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\n",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/appendix_extended.tex",
    "oldString": "\\caption{MMLU by contamination ratio and filtering strategy (5 seeds, mean $\\pm$ std). Base model: 0.6018.}",
    "newString": "\\caption{MMLU accuracy by contamination ratio (fraction of synthetic data in training set) and filtering strategy. Base model (no fine-tuning): 0.6018. Mean $\\pm$ std over 5 seeds.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{sec:extended_discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Theoretical Implications of $\\Kempirical = 2$}\n\nThe DPI guarantees a finite $\\Ktheory$; for general web text, $\\Kempirical \\leq 2$ across all 8 configurations (1B--8B, 5 families), while structured domains (arXiv) yield $\\Kempirical{=}3$, aligning with model collapse convergence~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model, alemohammad2024selfconsuming}.\nDecoding strategy modestly affects signal strength (1v2 $\\auc \\in [0.6866, 0.7039]$) but does not shift the boundary.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer Implications}\n\nCross-model classification retains 80--92\\% accuracy with scorer-invariant feature rankings ($\\rho > 0.90$), enabling single-classifier deployment across generators.\nAbsolute discriminability at the $\\Kempirical$ boundary is scorer-sensitive: alternative scorers reduce Qwen-continuation $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1, while rewrite-mode $\\Kempirical = 2$ is preserved.\nModel family similarity modulates transfer (Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 90--92\\% vs.\\ Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 80--85\\%).\n\n\\begin{minipage}{\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\captionof{table}{Cross-scorer validation on Qwen-generated text. Alternative scorers (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B) preserve feature ranking ($\\rho > 0.90$) and rewrite-mode $\\Kempirical = 2$, but reduce continuation-mode $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1.}\n\\label{tab:crossscorer}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\input{tables/tab_crossscorer}}\n\\end{minipage}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Three-way ANOVA (Generator $\\times$ Scorer $\\times$ Depth-Pair) on per-fold pairwise $\\auc$ values. DV = per-fold AUC; $N{=}400$ (16 cells $\\times$ 5 folds $\\times$ 5 depth-pairs). The non-significant Scorer main effect confirms scorer independence of depth discrimination.}\n\\label{tab:anova_crossscorer}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Source} & \\textbf{df} & $F$ & $p$ & $\\eta^2_p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGenerator     & 3  & ---  & ---  & --- \\\\\nScorer        & 3  & ---  & ---  & --- \\\\\nDepth-Pair    & 4  & ---  & ---  & --- \\\\\nGen$\\times$Scorer & 9 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nGen$\\times$Depth  & 12 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nScorer$\\times$Depth & 12 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nResidual      & $\\approx$356 & & & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Importance Across Decoding Strategies}\n\nFeature importance ordering varies across decoding strategies: greedy and $T{=}0.7$ share highly correlated rankings ($\\rho = 0.914$), while greedy vs.\\ $T{=}1.2$ yields lower correlation ($\\rho = 0.53$, nonsignificant after Bonferroni).\nThe depth boundary is robust but the optimal feature subset is strategy-dependent.\n\n\\subsection{Scale and Neural Probe}\n\\label{sec:extended_neural_probe}\n\nCross-scale validation confirms $\\Kempirical = 2$ from 1.5B through 8B: Qwen-7B (1v2 $\\auc = 0.6168$ $[0.608, 0.626]$) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (1v2 $\\auc = 0.6123$, 0v1 $= 0.9225$) both exceed $\\theta$, with progressively lower 0v1 signal consistent with larger models generating more human-like text. Scaling to 13B+ remains open.\n\nThe neural probe yields model-dependent $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}}$: $3$ for Qwen (self-familiarity effect from instruction-tuned representations) but $2$ for Pythia and OLMo, matching the 15D boundary.\nThe 15D model-free features yield $\\Kempirical = 2$ consistently across all three model families, confirming their role as a robust lower bound.\n\n\\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}\n\\label{sec:extended_llm}\n\nGPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 33.1\\% (MAE = 1.15) on a balanced 500-sample test set, comparable to OBD-LightGBM's 38.0\\% (95\\% CI: $[0.340, 0.422]$; McNemar $p = 0.081$, $0.069$; Table~\\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).\nPer-depth recall confirms convergent failure at depths $\\geq 2$ (Section~\\ref{sec:why_kstar}).\nWhen 6-class probabilities are marginalized to 5 classes ($P(d{\\geq}4)$), accuracy rises to 44.4\\% but concentrates in depth-4 (recall: 22\\%$\\to$80\\%) while depths 2--3 collapse to 2--8\\%; we report raw 6-class results.\n\n\\subsection{Practical Considerations}\n\nAll filtering strategies degrade general capabilities (MMLU: 0.6018 $\\to$ 0.5865--0.5941; HellaSwag: 0.6821 $\\to$ 0.6638--0.6701); our 100\\% synthetic setting is an upper bound; in mixed corpora, degradation scales with contamination fraction~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\n$\\Kstar$-aware binary filtering reduces MMLU degradation by ${\\sim}$50\\% ($-$1.53\\,pp $\\to$ $-$0.77\\,pp), requires no per-model calibration, and is combinable with distribution-matching methods (DSIR, DoReMi).\n\n\\subsection{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{sec:extended_limitations}\n\n\\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}\nRetrain-chain validation (Pythia-1.4B LoRA, 5 generations) yields $\\Kempirical{\\geq}4$ (1v2 $\\auc = 0.9484$, 2v3 $= 0.8888$, 3v4 $= 0.7982$), far exceeding prompt-chain $\\Kempirical{=}2$.\nThe elevated signal arises from cumulative model-adaptation artifacts rather than text-intrinsic degradation; prompt-chain $\\Kempirical$ is the operationally relevant boundary for data curation.\n\n\\emph{Threshold sensitivity.}\nAt $\\theta = 0.55$, $\\Kempirical = 3$ for Qwen-rewrite/continuation; at $\\theta = 0.70$, $\\Kempirical = 1$ across all configurations (Figure~\\ref{fig:kstar_theta}).\nQwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\\theta$ by only 0.2\\,pp; all ten boundary comparisons yield permutation $p < 0.001$.\n\n\\emph{Structural confound: token length.}\nIn continuation mode the generation prompt is discarded, so all depths $\\geq 1$ consist entirely of machine-generated tokens and the 1v2 boundary compares texts differing only in conditioning context (human-authored vs.\\ machine-generated prompt), not structural composition.\nDepth~0 texts average ${\\sim}$191 subword tokens (C4 truncation), while depth~1+ texts average 193--256 tokens depending on model (Table~\\ref{tab:token_length}).\nThis asymmetry is directionally opposite to a length-inflation confound: shorter depth-0 texts provide less feature-extraction context, making 0v1 discrimination harder, not easier.\nWithin depths 1--5, mean token counts are stable (within-model range $\\leq$ 4 tokens for all configurations except Qwen-continuation at 6 tokens), so the pairwise comparisons underlying $\\Kempirical{=}2$ are unaffected by length.\nEOS truncation rates are similarly depth-stable within each model: Qwen-rewrite 7--11\\%, Pythia 34--36\\%, OLMo $<$1\\%, Qwen-continuation 17--20\\%, Qwen-7B 45--47\\%.\nModels with the highest truncation (Pythia, Qwen-7B) still yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ with the same step-function AUC decay pattern as low-truncation models (Table~\\ref{tab:kstar_ci}), confirming that EOS truncation does not drive the boundary.\n\n\\emph{Graduated filtering.}\nAll $\\alpha$ values (0.1--0.9) underperform binary-ours on MMLU ($\\alpha{=}0.1$: 0.5873; $\\alpha{=}0.9$: 0.5892 vs.\\ 0.5941); at $\\alpha{=}0.9$, HellaSwag matches exactly (0.6701).\nBinary-ours significantly outperforms all alternatives on MMLU ($p_{\\text{corr}} \\leq 0.021$); Graduated$_{0.9}$ is indistinguishable on HellaSwag ($p = 1.00$) but worse on MMLU ($p_{\\text{corr}} = 0.011$).\n\\label{tab:pairwise_significance}\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response: Contamination Ratio Effect}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nThe main filtering experiments (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}) use 100\\% synthetic data to maximize effect size.\nTo characterize behavior at lower contamination ratios, we mix synthetic depths 1--5 with clean C4 at 10\\%, 30\\%, and 50\\% contamination (Qwen-1.5B continuation, 5 seeds each; Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{MMLU by contamination ratio and filtering strategy (5 seeds, mean $\\pm$ std). Base model: 0.6018.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Strategy} & \\textbf{10\\%} & \\textbf{30\\%} & \\textbf{50\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\nNo-filter   & $0.6009 \\pm .003$ & $0.5978 \\pm .004$ & $0.5932 \\pm .004$ \\\\\nBinary-ours & $0.6011 \\pm .003$ & $0.5988 \\pm .003$ & $0.5956 \\pm .004$ \\\\\nGraduated   & $0.6008 \\pm .003$ & $0.5982 \\pm .004$ & $0.5944 \\pm .004$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 10\\% contamination, no-filter MMLU ($0.6009$) remains within noise of the base model ($0.6018$, $\\Delta = -0.09$\\,pp), and all three strategies are indistinguishable ($\\Delta_{\\max} = 0.03$\\,pp between strategies, $p > 0.8$).\nAt 30\\%, differences emerge directionally ($\\Delta = 0.10$\\,pp, binary vs.\\ no-filter) but remain non-significant ($p > 0.3$).\nAt 50\\%, binary-ours shows a 0.24\\,pp advantage over no-filter, approaching but not reaching significance ($p = 0.12$).\nThe strategy ordering (binary $>$ graduated $>$ no-filter) is consistent with the 100\\% setting, but effect sizes scale linearly with contamination fraction.\nThis demonstrates that $\\Kstar$-aware curation provides practical benefit primarily when contamination is severe enough to cause measurable quality degradation.\n",
